❯ why do you think we need to ask the opticians whether they agree to
  reminders? What could make them not agree? Worst case, a customer might
  wander into the optician's store because we reminded them and ask for another
  test and the opticians know nothing about a reminder. That is not ideal, but
  is it critical, will the opticians complain?

⏺ Mostly no: your worst case isn't critical, and I'd make the optician step much
  lighter. But we can't skip their agreement entirely, for a different reason
  than the one you describe.

  Why some form of agreement is needed: it's the rule, not the risk of a walk-in
  - [CONSTRAINTS] say Mirantus may contact customers "only for the service
    itself, or on the optician's behalf and with the optician's name on it".
  - The service messages are listed there: booking confirmation, appointment
    reminder, "your result is ready". A reminder a year later isn't one of them,
    so it has to go out on the optician's behalf.
  - Mailing customers in someone's name without their agreement isn't "on their
    behalf"; it's using their name. Two opticians have said plainly they don't
    want this ([SURVEY-4], [INT-03]). If one of them found such a mail in a
    customer's inbox, they would complain, about the mail itself rather than the
    walk-in.

  Your worst case is mild. A customer who comes back through the booking link
  creates an ordinary mira booking at their store, which opticians handle every
  day. A walk-in asking for a screening is normal too. The real failure cases
  are narrower:
  - The store can't screen any more. INT-03's second store had no trained
    examiner for six weeks. A reminder then sends the customer into a store that
    has to turn them away.
  - Chains. "Our chain HQ decides about customer communication, not the store."
    [SURVEY-7] A chain will likely object to mail in its brand that its HQ never
    approved. That could become a contract issue, not just a grumble.
  - Opticians who object to the principle ([SURVEY-4], [INT-03]). That's two
    voices out of 16 sources, so it is not necessarily common.

  What I'd do instead of a 15-optician opt-in gate
  - Independent stores: notify and allow opt-out. All partners get a notice
    ("from [date] your customers who agreed get one reminder in your name; opt
    out here"). We measure the opt-out rate instead of chasing signatures. This
    is cheap, it gives every optician a say, and nobody is surprised by the
    walk-ins.
  - Chains: opt-in via HQ, because they explicitly control customer
    communication.
  - The 2-week test: simply run it at 4 stores we already know are willing.
    There's no need to make acceptance a gate before the test.
  - Legal check in week 0: whether an opt-out notice counts as acting "on the
    optician's behalf". That is an [ASSUMPTION] until legal confirms it.

  Shall I change the memo and the spec accordingly? That means opt-out for
  independents, opt-in for chains, opt-out rate as a guardrail, and the
  15-optician gate removed.

❯ maybe we can do it with even less changes. We have the report e-mail already.
  We add a field where the doctors must enter a recommended timeframe for the
  next screening. We will calculate the date and IN the mail with the report we
  add a button that users can click that will send them a reminder mail from
  Mirantus. So we have to ask nobody and by clicking the button we already got
  the consent from the user to send them an e-mail
⏺ Your version solves consent and the medical side cleanly. The one thing it
  doesn't solve is the sender: a reminder from Mirantus breaks the rule on who
  owns the customer, and the customer's click doesn't change that.

  What works
  - Consent. Asking in the result e-mail is explicitly allowed: "Consent can be
    asked for again later, but only in a message the customer already receives
    legitimately" [CONSTRAINTS]. The click is the consent, so we don't need to
    ask anyone else. Whether we also need a confirmation e-mail is a minor legal
    detail.
  - Medical. The timeframe comes from the doctor's evaluation, so computing a
    date from it is fine. The e-mail button just says "Erinnern Sie mich an mein
    nächstes Screening" [remind me of my next screening] and doesn't state the
    interval.
  - Less work. One form field, one button, one stored date, one reminder mail.
    No opt-in round with opticians, no DNS setup.

  What doesn't: the sender
  - [CONSTRAINTS], "The optician owns the relationship": "Mirantus may contact
    customers only for the service itself, or on the optician's behalf and with
    the optician's name on it."
  - A reminder a year later isn't "the service itself". The rules list the
    service messages: booking confirmation, appointment reminder, "your result
    is ready". A Mirantus-branded reminder is therefore not allowed. The
    customer's consent covers data protection (GDPR); it doesn't override
    Mirantus's contract with the optician.
  - It is also exactly what the objecting opticians named: "Bitte keine Mails
    von Mirantus an meine Kunden" [Please no e-mails from Mirantus to my
    customers] [SURVEY-4]. The interviewed optician said she doesn't want
    customers "getting e-mails from a company they've never heard of"
    (translated) [INT-03]. These two are the ones who would complain.
  - Customers might actually prefer your version. The Berlin customer wants the
    reminder "not from the optician … from whoever has my results" [INT-02]. The
    block is the contract, not the customer.

  Cheapest version that stays inside the rules

  Keep your flow exactly, with one change: the reminder carries the optician's
  name. It is still sent from the Mirantus domain and still has the "remind me"
  button. The sender line reads "Optik Muster <erinnerung@…mirantus…>", signed
  "Ihr Team von Optik Muster". On top of that:
  - Independent stores: a one-time notice with an opt-out, which is how we act
    "on their behalf". No signatures to chase, no DNS, no work for them.
  - Chain stores: opt-in via head office [SURVEY-7].
  - Legal, in week 0: two questions.
    - Does an opt-out notice count as acting "on behalf"?
    - Could a reminder the customer asks for themselves count as part of "the
      service"? If legal says yes, your Mirantus-branded version works as is.

  Small addition I'd keep: one line in the report PDF naming the doctor's
  recommendation. The rules want the doctor's recommendation in the report. It
  also means customers who don't click still learn when to come back.

  Shall I update the memo and spec to this version? It would be your flow, plus
  the optician's name on the reminder, opt-out for independent stores, opt-in
  for chains, and the report line.

❯ ok, your cheapest version sounds like a good start and yes, mention the
  recommeded date also in the report

› Message from reviewer
  Review of
  /Users/simondeichsel/gits/mirantus-case-2nd-run/outputs/04-decision-memo.md
  and
  /Users/simondeichsel/gits/mirantus-case-2nd-run/outputs/05-prototype-spec.md.
  I re-read data/constraints.md for this review. `python3
  scripts/check_outputs.py` passed with no failures. I did not flag the
  German-only choice, because Simon decided it (CP2 addendum 2).

  **BLOCKING**

  1. **Memo, "2-week test", sample size.** The 01 analysis gives 9000 screenings
  a year across 60 locations, roughly 12–13 per location per month (INT-03 says
  about 30 a month across 2 stores). So 4 stores over 2 weeks yield only a few
  dozen result e-mails. The decision rule ("20% of recipients click") and the
  split "by age band and booking consent" cannot be read at that n; under
  CLAUDE.md every cell would be directional. Fix: add expected test n to
  `scripts/analyze.py` (screenings per location per 2 weeks). Then either widen
  the test to all willing stores, or restate the rule with a minimum n and label
  it directional. A short qualitative add-on (calling a few clickers and
  non-clickers) would also help.

  2. **Memo, "2-week test", "existing product".** The live v0 is items 1–3 plus
  S2 and click storage: a new form field, a new PDF line, a button, a
  confirmation page and a consent record. None of that exists today. Week 0
  covers only approvals, and build time is not scheduled anywhere. CLAUDE.md
  requires a test that "can actually run in two weeks with the existing
  product." Fix: either say the build happens before the clock starts and how
  long it takes, or define a v0 on the existing product. One option: the doctor
  writes the interval as a fixed line in the assessment text (your own
  fallback), and the button links to a simple landing page that logs the click
  and the consent.

  **SHOULD**

  3. **Constraints, Medical advice: spec M2, "Dieser Zeitpunkt ist jetzt
  erreicht."** [This time has now come.] The rule says an interval for a
  specific customer "must come from the ophthalmologist's evaluation, not from
  marketing copy." M2 attributes the timing to the doctor, but it still gives
  the interval outside the report, next to a booking push. Fix: add a [Befund
  ansehen] link so the recommendation itself stays in the report. Keep this on
  the week-0 legal list, as the spec already does.

  4. **Consent: spec M1, wording at the button.** The customer only learns they
  can withdraw on S2, after clicking. Fix: next to the button, add "Sie können
  die Erinnerung jederzeit abbestellen" [you can cancel the reminder at any
  time], plus a short note that the reminder uses the date from the report. Keep
  "keine Werbung" [no advertising], and make sure M2 never carries offers.

  5. **Relationship: spec M1 sender vs. the optician notice.** M1, the service
  e-mail, now also goes out under the optician's name ("{Optiker} <befund@…>").
  The memo does not mention this change, and the §3 notice only tells opticians
  about the reminder. Fix: list the sender change in memo item 3 and in the
  notice text, or keep M1's sender as it is today.

  6. **Relationship: memo "Why this," bullet 2.** "It fits the rules … the
  reminder carries the optician's name [CONSTRAINTS]" overstates the case:
  whether an opt-out notice counts as "on the optician's behalf" is still open
  for legal. Fix: add "pending legal (week 0)". In the decision rule, also say
  what happens if legal says no (switch to opt-in, as CHECKPOINTS already
  records).

  7. **Evidence: memo cut list, "opticians object [SURVEY-4]."** This rests on
  one of 15 survey answers. Fix: write "one optician objects [SURVEY-4]; see
  also [INT-03]."

  8. **Evidence: strongest alternative not addressed.** Phone follow-up is
  associated with 8.2% (n=452), 9.7% after location standardisation. This is the
  largest association in the data, and [SURVEY-3] and [INT-03] both ask for a
  due-list. The memo cites only the e-mail figure (4.6% vs 4.0%). Fix: in one
  clause, give the phone association and why it is not first: self-reported,
  mostly 6 independent stores plus 1 chain with n=33, and it costs staff time
  [SURVEY-9]. Note that [SURVEY-3] and [SURVEY-9] contradict each other.

  9. **Evidence: an unnamed contradiction.** [INT-02]: "But not from the
  optician. I don't even remember which store it was." [SURVEY-11] makes the
  same point about memory. The reminder is sent in the optician's name, but the
  memo never mentions this tension. Fix: name it in the trade-off. Part of the
  mitigation is already in M2, which names the screening date and mira.

  10. **Trade-off missing reach costs.** The memo leaves out two reach costs:
      - The button sits in a German result e-mail. Non-German customers open at
  37.8% vs 71.8% (non-de n=1633, de n=7367).
      - Chain customers get nothing until HQ opts in.

      Fix: one sentence in "Trade-off."

  11. **Metric: no power check for +2pp.** A 20% holdout, restricted to the
  12-month group, may not detect +2pp on a baseline of about 5% within a year's
  cohort. Fix: add a sample-size calculation to the analysis script and state
  the readout date the target implies. Also say whether the comparison is by
  assignment (button shown vs. not), which is what it should be.

  12. **Metric: untagged decisions.** The "+3 months" grace period, the 12/24/36
  options and the "4 stores" are decisions or assumptions with no tag. Fix: tag
  "+3 months" [TARGET] (justified by [CSV] spread), the 12/24/36 options
  [INT-04], and "4 willing stores" [ASSUMPTION].

  13. **Guardrails: interval inflation.** Nothing checks whether doctors default
  everyone to 12 months, which would raise returns but not medical soundness
  ([INT-04]). Fix: add the interval distribution by age and result as a
  guardrail.

  14. **Memo/spec consistency.** Each of these is in the spec but missing from
  the memo:
      - The spec commits "Everyone who clicks during the test gets M2, even if
  we stop."
      - Memo item 3, "every customer with a numeric interval," leaves out the
  holdout, opted-out stores and chains that have not opted in, all of which the
  spec lists.
      - "Items 1–3" leaves out S2 and click storage, which the test needs.

      Fix: align with one line each.

  15. **Spec §3: chain HQ variant not written.** "Same text … as opt-in" leaves
  "Wenn Sie das nicht möchten" [if you don't want this] wrong for HQ. Fix: write
  the HQ copy in full and check it. Also clarify "Antworten auf die E-Mail"
  [replies to the e-mail] to mean "Antworten Ihrer Kundinnen und Kunden auf die
  Erinnerung" [your customers' replies to the reminder]. Mention the 20% holdout
  so opticians are not surprised.

  16. **Spec §5, Claude Design prompt.**
      - It says "Use ONLY the German copy in section 2," but the third entry
  needs the section 3 notice. Change it to "sections 2 and 3."
      - The start screen does not show a customer with and a customer without
  booking consent, as CLAUDE.md requires. Label the two paths, for example
  "mit/ohne Einwilligung bei Buchung – gleicher Ablauf" [with/without consent at
  booking – same flow].

  17. **Memo length.** It is 595 words: within the hard limit of 600 but well
  over the ≈500 target, so the fixes above have no room. Fix: cut "What ships
  first" item 6 and the cut-list wording to make space.

  Sign-off: no. The 2-week test as written cannot produce a readable result (too
  few users) and cannot run on the existing product without a build, so I would
  not sign it off until findings 1–2 are fixed.

❯ yes, let's use the maximum test size that we can get without much hassle. The
  opt-out solution should work fine - maybe we can even do this for all POVs

⏺ Yes. For chains the notice goes to head office, which "decides about customer
  communication, not the store" [SURVEY-7]. Store staff couldn't respond to it
  anyway. An opt-out via head office is the same legal construction as for
  independent stores, so the week-0 legal check covers both.

  The stakes are higher, though. One chain head office opting out or complaining
  affects many locations at once, and chains usually have brand and legal
  teams. So the partner manager should also call each chain head office briefly,
  on top of the written notice. That is a handful of calls, not an opt-in
  round. With all stores, the test reaches about n=330 result e-mails in two
  weeks [CSV]. Mobile units stay out, since no source tells us whose name such a
  reminder would carry.

  I'll apply that now.

    weitere Nachricht" [no further message].

  - The optician notices use a sample start date, "ab 1.12.2026" [from
    1.12.2026].

    prototype.
